# CRAMP1-dependent histone H1 biogenesis is essential for topoisomerase II inhibitor tolerance.
**Authors:** Ingham A, de Vega IA, Morlot L, Gittens W, Hendriks IA, Kakulidis ES, Freire R, Davey NE, Duxin JP, Lund Nielsen M, Mailand N
**Journal:** Mol Cell (2025)
**DOI:** [10.1016/j.molcel.2025.04.006](https://doi.org/10.1016/j.molcel.2025.04.006)
**PMC:** [PMC12240685](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC12240685/)

## Abstract

1. Mol Cell. 2025 Jul 3;85(13):2487-2502.e12. doi: 10.1016/j.molcel.2025.04.006.
Epub 2025 Jun 13.

CRAMP1-dependent histone H1 biogenesis is essential for topoisomerase II
inhibitor tolerance.

Topoisomerase II (TOP2) inhibitors (TOP2i) are mainstay chemotherapeutic agents
that undermine genome integrity by stabilizing TOP2-DNA complexes accompanied by
DNA damage formation. Here, we reveal the uncharacterized protein CRAMP1 and H1
linker histones as key effectors of TOP2i tolerance in human cells. We
demonstrate that CRAMP1 defines a dedicated histone H1 biogenesis factor
stimulating transcription of both replicative and non-replicative H1 genes,
driven by its concurrent targeting to histone gene loci and H1-specific promoter
motifs. CRAMP1 promotes TOP2i tolerance by maintaining H1 supply, involving a
novel mechanism uncoupled from TOP2i-induced DNA damage whereby reducing the H1
pool triggers unscheduled TOP2 substrate formation in low-accessibility
chromatin states. This amplifies total demand for TOP2 activity, lowering the
threshold for TOP2i-mediated exhaustion of TOP2. Our discoveries elucidate the
mechanistic basis of histone H1 biogenesis in human cells, opening opportunities
for selectively manipulating linker but not core histone supply and targeting
cancer-associated H1 deficiency.

## Full Text

IntroductionGenomic DNA exists in the state of chromatin, wrapped around core histones to form nucleosomes, the basic repeat unit of genome organization.1,2 Chromatin structure is stabilized by H1 linker histones, which are highly abundant chromatin constituents that cooperatively bind nucleosomes and the intervening linker DNA, regulating nucleosome spacing and promoting chromatin compaction.3,4,5 A range of histone H1 isoforms (approximately 10 in humans) are expressed in cells, but the precise functions of individual variants are not well understood.3,4,6 Proper organization of chromatin architecture is fundamental to all aspects of genome maintenance, utilization, and transmission. As histones represent the fundamental building blocks of chromatin, maintaining an ample histone supply is of critical importance, particularly during genome duplication when millions of new copies of all histones must be synthesized to package the newly replicated DNA.7 To accomplish this task, cells not only encode non-replicative histone genes that are expressed constitutively during the cell cycle (primarily H1.0 and H1.10 in the case of linker histones) but also replication-dependent histone genes (H1.1–H1.5 for linker histones). Unlike most other mRNAs, replicative histone genes lack introns and do not undergo poly-adenylation but instead end in a conserved stem-loop structure.8 These replicative histone genes cluster in evolutionarily conserved arrays comprising up to 50 interspersed core and linker histone genes,9 enabling their co-regulated expression coinciding with the onset of DNA replication.10 Together with a small number of known factors dedicated to replicative histone gene transcription and pre-mRNA processing, including the scaffolding protein NPAT11,12,13 and the stem-loop-processing factors FLASH and SLBP that recruit specialized spliceosome components,14,15,16,17 these histone gene arrays assemble into compartmentalized subnuclear structures known as histone locus bodies (HLBs).18 HLBs thus define the sites of coordinated replicative histone mRNA biogenesis that is essential for cell proliferation.Despite the importance of histones in genome organization and utilization, insights into the factors and mechanisms that promote and regulate histone production in vertebrates remain limited. This is true in particular for linker histones. Here, we unveil the mechanistic basis of histone H1 biogenesis in human cells, driven by the uncharacterized protein CRAMP1 that we show acts as a transcription factor for both replicative and non-replicative H1 isoforms but not core histones. We reveal that CRAMP1-dependent maintenance of H1 supply governs the demand for topoisomerase II (TOP2) action and is therefore of central importance for the cellular response to TOP2 inhibitors (TOP2i), which are among the most widely used cancer chemotherapeutic agents in the clinic.19 TOP2 enzymes resolve DNA catenanes and supercoils by cleaving one DNA duplex and passing another duplex through it before resealing the break, and TOP2i disrupts this catalytic cycle, leading to stabilization of highly cytotoxic TOP2-DNA complexes.19,20 We establish that CRAMP1-dependent histone H1 biogenesis exerts protection against TOP2i via a conceptually novel mechanism uncoupled from TOP2i-induced DNA damage, suppressing the formation of TOP2 substrates in low-accessibility chromatin states; consequently, diminishing the H1 pool exacerbates demand for TOP2 activity, hypersensitizing cells to TOP2 inhibition. These findings open new avenues for rectifying the currently limited insights into H1 biological functions via manipulation of linker histone supply and suggest opportunities for exploiting defective H1 status that is prevalent in common cancers, including lymphomas21,22 in therapeutic strategies targeting elevated TOP2 demand.

Introduction

Genomic DNA exists in the state of chromatin, wrapped around core histones to form nucleosomes, the basic repeat unit of genome organization.1,2 Chromatin structure is stabilized by H1 linker histones, which are highly abundant chromatin constituents that cooperatively bind nucleosomes and the intervening linker DNA, regulating nucleosome spacing and promoting chromatin compaction.3,4,5 A range of histone H1 isoforms (approximately 10 in humans) are expressed in cells, but the precise functions of individual variants are not well understood.3,4,6 Proper organization of chromatin architecture is fundamental to all aspects of genome maintenance, utilization, and transmission. As histones represent the fundamental building blocks of chromatin, maintaining an ample histone supply is of critical importance, particularly during genome duplication when millions of new copies of all histones must be synthesized to package the newly replicated DNA.7 To accomplish this task, cells not only encode non-replicative histone genes that are expressed constitutively during the cell cycle (primarily H1.0 and H1.10 in the case of linker histones) but also replication-dependent histone genes (H1.1–H1.5 for linker histones). Unlike most other mRNAs, replicative histone genes lack introns and do not undergo poly-adenylation but instead end in a conserved stem-loop structure.8 These replicative histone genes cluster in evolutionarily conserved arrays comprising up to 50 interspersed core and linker histone genes,9 enabling their co-regulated expression coinciding with the onset of DNA replication.10 Together with a small number of known factors dedicated to replicative histone gene transcription and pre-mRNA processing, including the scaffolding protein NPAT11,12,13 and the stem-loop-processing factors FLASH and SLBP that recruit specialized spliceosome components,14,15,16,17 these histone gene arrays assemble into compartmentalized subnuclear structures known as histone locus bodies (HLBs).18 HLBs thus define the sites of coordinated replicative histone mRNA biogenesis that is essential for cell proliferation.

Despite the importance of histones in genome organization and utilization, insights into the factors and mechanisms that promote and regulate histone production in vertebrates remain limited. This is true in particular for linker histones. Here, we unveil the mechanistic basis of histone H1 biogenesis in human cells, driven by the uncharacterized protein CRAMP1 that we show acts as a transcription factor for both replicative and non-replicative H1 isoforms but not core histones. We reveal that CRAMP1-dependent maintenance of H1 supply governs the demand for topoisomerase II (TOP2) action and is therefore of central importance for the cellular response to TOP2 inhibitors (TOP2i), which are among the most widely used cancer chemotherapeutic agents in the clinic.19 TOP2 enzymes resolve DNA catenanes and supercoils by cleaving one DNA duplex and passing another duplex through it before resealing the break, and TOP2i disrupts this catalytic cycle, leading to stabilization of highly cytotoxic TOP2-DNA complexes.19,20 We establish that CRAMP1-dependent histone H1 biogenesis exerts protection against TOP2i via a conceptually novel mechanism uncoupled from TOP2i-induced DNA damage, suppressing the formation of TOP2 substrates in low-accessibility chromatin states; consequently, diminishing the H1 pool exacerbates demand for TOP2 activity, hypersensitizing cells to TOP2 inhibition. These findings open new avenues for rectifying the currently limited insights into H1 biological functions via manipulation of linker histone supply and suggest opportunities for exploiting defective H1 status that is prevalent in common cancers, including lymphomas21,22 in therapeutic strategies targeting elevated TOP2 demand.

ResultsCRAMP1 is required for resistance to TOP2iTo discover factors protecting against TOP2i cytotoxicity, we performed parallel genome-scale CRISPR-Cas9 gene inactivation screens in human U2OS osteosarcoma cells for synthetic lethality relationships with the TOP2 poison etoposide and the catalytic TOP2i ICRF-193, which trap TOP2 on DNA in a covalent and non-covalent manner, respectively (Figure 1A).20 Along with well-established repair factors for trapped TOP2 complexes, including ZNF451 and TDP2,20 these screens revealed genes encoding the uncharacterized protein CRAMP1 and the histone H1 isoform H1.2 among a small subset displaying synthetic lethal interactions with both etoposide and ICRF-193 (Figures 1A–1C; Table S1). Additional H1 isoforms, including H1.0 and H1.4, scored just below the significance threshold (Figure 1B; Table S1). Targeted knockout (KO) of CRAMP1 in U2OS or HeLa cells, which did not alter cell cycle distribution in unstressed cells, recapitulated the strong hypersensitivity to etoposide and ICRF-193 resulting from CRAMP1 loss observed in the screens (Figures 1D and S1A–S1F). Importantly, this hypersensitivity could be fully rescued by stable complementation with wild-type (WT) GFP-tagged CRAMP1 (Figure 1E). Moreover, KO or small interfering RNA (siRNA)-mediated depletion of CRAMP1 selectively hypersensitized cells to TOP2i but not to a range of other genotoxins, including the topoisomerase 1 (TOP1) inhibitor camptothecin (Figures 1D, S1D, S1G, and S1H). TOP2 poisons, including etoposide and doxorubicin, potently trigger DNA double-strand break (DSB) formation by stabilizing covalent TOP2 cleavage complexes (TOP2ccs).20 Surprisingly, however, while CRAMP1-KO cells accumulated significantly elevated amounts of etoposide-induced DSBs as evidenced by γH2AX foci formation and neutral comet assays, they repaired these lesions with similar kinetics to that of parental cells, unlike depletion of TDP2, which significantly delayed etoposide-induced DSB clearance as expected23 (Figures 1F and S2A–S2F). This suggests that CRAMP1 does not function as a conventional repair factor for TOP2ccs. Indeed, CRAMP1 KO was non-epistatic with ZNF451 loss in promoting etoposide tolerance (Figures 1G and S2G). Despite CRAMP1-KO cells being proficient for resolution of etoposide-induced DSBs, they displayed an increased accumulation in G2 phase, a marked increase in the proportion of enlarged cells, and elevated p21 expression relative to their WT counterparts after etoposide treatment, reminiscent of a senescence-like state (Figures 1H, 1I, and S3A–S3C). DSB formation in human cells triggers a signaling cascade initiated by the ataxia telangiectasia mutated (ATM) kinase.24 Interestingly, however, etoposide-treated CRAMP1-KO cells showed a persistent increase in ATR-dependent RPA foci formation and RPA2 S33 phosphorylation but not markers of ATM signaling, including CHK2 T68 and KAP1 S824 phosphorylation (Figures 1J and S3D–S3F). ATR inhibition fully suppressed the etoposide-induced enlargement of CRAMP1-KO cells and increased occurrence of RPA foci (Figures 1I, 1J, and S3G). Moreover, CRAMP1-KO led to an increased level of mitotic DNA synthesis indicative of DNA under-replication25 after etoposide treatment (Figures S3H and S3I), suggesting that CRAMP1 deficiency induces a failure to complete DNA replication following exposure to etoposide, triggering sustained ATR-dependent cell cycle arrest in G2 phase. CRAMP1 KO likewise exacerbated G2 arrest and accumulation of large cells upon treatment with ICRF-193 that does not generate TOP2ccs (Figures S3J and S3K), substantiating that CRAMP1 does not act as a repair factor for these lesions. Together, these data show that CRAMP1 has a critical role in promoting resistance to TOP2i but is dispensable for resolving DNA damage induced by these agents.Figure 1CRAMP1 is required for TOP2i resistance but not repair of TOP2i-induced DNA damage(A) Schematic outline of genome-scale CRISPR-Cas9 screens for genes whose KO sensitizes cells to etoposide or ICRF-193. NormZ values of <−3 and >3 were used as cutoffs for defining significant gene KOs conferring hypersensitivity and resistance, respectively, to the drugs. See Table S1 for full results.(C) Venn diagram of significant genes (NormZ < −3) from DrugZ analysis of CRISPR screens in (B).(D) Sulforhodamine B (SRB) cell growth assays using U2OS WT and derivative CRAMP1-KO cell lines treated with various doses of TOP2i (etoposide, ICRF-193, and doxorubicin; top) or other genotoxic agents (bottom) (mean ± SEM; n = 3 independent experiments).(E) SRB cell growth assays using U2OS WT, CRAMP1-KO, and CRAMP1-KO/CRAMP1-GFP cell lines treated with various doses of etoposide (mean ± SEM; n = 3 independent experiments).(F) Quantitative immunofluorescence analysis of γH2AX mean intensity in U2OS WT and CRAMP1-KO cells treated with etoposide (20 μM) for 1 h and collected at indicated times (mean ± SEM; n = 5 independent experiments; at least 5,000 cells analyzed per condition; unpaired two-tailed t test).(G) SRB cell growth assays using U2OS WT and CRAMP1-KO cells transfected with non-targeting control (CTRL) or ZNF451 siRNAs and treated with indicated doses of etoposide (mean ± SEM; n = 3 independent experiments).(H) Representative images of U2OS WT and CRAMP1-KO cells treated or not with etoposide (2 μM) for 1 h and fixed 48 h later. Scale bar, 10 μM.(I) Quantification of nuclear area in U2OS WT and CRAMP1-KO cells that were left untreated or exposed to etoposide (2 μM) for 1 h, then collected immediately or grown for an additional 48 h in the absence or presence of ATR inhibitor (ATRi; 1 μM) (mean ± SEM; n = 3 independent experiments; at least 2,000 cells analyzed per condition; unpaired two-tailed t test). The maximal nuclear area in untreated cells was used as a cutoff to determine the proportion of cells with increased nuclear size.(J) Quantification of RPA2 foci in G2 phase cells in (I) immunostained with RPA2 antibody (mean ± SEM; n = 3 independent experiments; at least 500 cells analyzed per condition; unpaired two-tailed t test).∗∗∗p < 0.001, ∗∗p < 0.01, ∗p < 0.05; ns, non-significant.CRAMP1 is an H1-specific histone biogenesis factorTo understand how CRAMP1 protects against TOP2i cytotoxicity, we first used mass spectrometry (MS) to profile the CRAMP1 interactome, using cells stably complemented with CRAMP1-GFP expressed at an approximately 8-fold higher level than endogenous CRAMP1 (Figures 2A and S4A). This revealed the poorly characterized protein GON4L as the most highly enriched candidate CRAMP1 interactor, which we validated in co-immunoprecipitation (coIP) assays (Figures 2B, S4B, and S4C; Table S2). GON4L has been linked to HLBs via interaction with NPAT,26,27 which was also significantly enriched in the CRAMP1 interactome along with FLASH (Figure 2B), suggesting an involvement of CRAMP1 in histone synthesis. This notion was reinforced by DepMap portal data showing a co-dependency signature for CRAMP1 that included linker histone isoforms and the histone chaperones ASF1 and HIRA (Figure S4D). Indeed, stably expressed CRAMP1-GFP showed distinctive enrichment in NPAT-positive HLBs throughout interphase (Figures 2C and 2D). CRAMP1 recruitment to HLBs was dependent on both GON4L and the HLB core components NPAT and FLASH, whereas CRAMP1 KO did not impair NPAT and FLASH association with HLBs (Figures 2C, 2D, and S4E–S4J). We thus asked whether CRAMP1 might be required for the expression of histone genes. Unexpectedly, considering its HLB enrichment, CRAMP1 KO markedly reduced the transcript and protein abundance of replicative linker histone isoforms including H1.2 and H1.4 but not core histones (Figures 2E–2H and S5A–S5C). By contrast, knockdown of NPAT or FLASH diminished both replicative H1 and core histone transcript levels as expected, while the impact of GON4L loss on histone biogenesis could not be rigorously assessed due to a drastic reduction of cell fitness upon siRNA-mediated depletion of GON4L that may reflect its common essentiality according to the DepMap portal (Figures S5D–S5G). Furthermore, CRAMP1 loss impaired both replicative and non-replicative H1 isoform expression, with the notable exception of H1.1, whose transcript and protein abundance were moderately upregulated in CRAMP1-KO cells (see below) (Figures 2E–2H, S5A, S5C, and S5H). The downregulation of H1 expression in CRAMP1-deficient cells was mirrored by a concomitant decline in the levels of chromatin-bound H1, as expected (Figures S5I and S5J). Across all histone transcripts, CRAMP1 ablation led to an approximately 50% reduction in total H1 expression but had no overt impact on core histone levels, paralleling MS-based quantification of histone protein abundance (Figures 2H, S5K, and S5L). Importantly, along with a number of other genes, defective expression of H1 isoforms in CRAMP1-KO cells was rescued by stable complementation with WT ectopic CRAMP1 (Figures 2I and S5M). We conclude from these findings that CRAMP1 defines a histone H1 biogenesis factor in human cells.Figure 2CRAMP1 is a histone H1 biogenesis factor(A) Schematic outline of MS-based analysis of CRAMP1-GFP-interacting proteins.(B) MS analysis of GFP pulldowns from U2OS Flp-In T-REx CRAMP1-KO (KO) cells stably reconstituted or not with CRAMP1-GFP (CRAMP1-KO/CRAMP1-GFP). Volcano plot shows the mean difference of the protein intensity plotted against the p value (two-tailed two-sample Student’s t test). Asterisk denotes an unspecific band.(H) Scatterplot of the pooled peptide abundance of core and linker histones (x axis) from MS/MS analysis in WT/CRAMP1-KO vs. the pooled transcript abundance (TPM) of core and linker histones from RNA-seq (y axis) in WT/CRAMP1-KO.(I) RNA-seq scatterplot and Venn diagram of the log2 fold change of genes significantly altered in WT vs. CRAMP1-KO and CRAMP1-KO vs. CRAMP1-KO/CRAMP1-GFP cell lines. Differentially expressed genes were defined as having log2 fold change ≥ 0.58 and adjusted p > 0.05 (n = 3 technical replicates).∗∗∗p < 0.001, ∗∗p < 0.01, ∗p < 0.05; ns, non-significant.Histone H1 biogenesis is mediated by a CRAMP1-GON4L-NPAT pathwayWe next addressed the mechanistic basis of CRAMP1-dependent H1 biogenesis. AlphaFold structure modeling predicted two folded domains in human CRAMP1, comprising an N-terminal putative SANT/Myb domain found in many transcriptional regulators and chromatin-binding proteins and a domain of unknown function that we refer to as GON4L binding (GB) composed of bipartite folds (GB-N and GB-C) separated by an extended linker (Figures 3A and S6A). Importantly, stable complementation of CRAMP1-KO cells with CRAMP1-GFP deletion mutants lacking the SANT/Myb domain (ΔSANT) or individual lobes of the GB domain (ΔGB-N or ΔGB-C) established that both domains are essential for CRAMP1-mediated H1 biogenesis (Figures 3B and S6B–S6D). Disrupting GB domain integrity abrogated CRAMP1 enrichment at HLBs, whereas deletion of the SANT/Myb domain had no effect (Figures 3C and 3D). In agreement with the GON4L dependency for CRAMP1 recruitment to HLBs, AlphaFold-Multimer analysis suggested with high confidence that the CRAMP1 GB domain interacts with a C-terminal portion of GON4L, involving an extensive binding interface (Figures 3E and S7A). Consistently, coIP analysis confirmed that the CRAMP1-GON4L interaction is mediated by these regions (Figures 3F, S7B, and S7C). Further in silico modeling suggested that GON4L directly bridges CRAMP1 with NPAT (Figures 3G, S7D, and S7E). This was corroborated by MS data showing selective dropout of GON4L and NPAT from the CRAMP1 interactome upon deletion of the GB but not the SANT/Myb domain (Figures 3H and S7F; Table S2), providing a mechanistic explanation for GON4L- and NPAT-dependent recruitment of CRAMP1 to HLBs via the GB domain. These findings show that CRAMP1 interaction with GON4L-NPAT and HLBs is essential for its role in stimulating H1 biogenesis.Figure 3Histone H1 biogenesis is mediated by a CRAMP1-GON4L-NPAT pathway(A) AlphaFold2 model (top) and domain structure (bottom) of human CRAMP1. Volcano plot shows the mean difference of the protein intensity plotted against the p value (two-tailed two-sample Student’s t test). Significant differences (q value < 0.05) were calculated as in Figure 2B (n = 4 biological replicates).See Table S2 for full results. ∗∗∗p < 0.001; ns, non-significant.CRAMP1 acts as a histone H1 transcription factorBecause the CRAMP1 ΔSANT mutant showed intact recruitment to HLBs yet failed to restore expression of H1 isoforms in CRAMP1-KO cells, we speculated that the SANT/Myb domain might specifically target CRAMP1 to H1 gene loci. To test this, we used CUT&RUN to profile genome-wide DNA interactions of stably expressed GFP-CRAMP1 proteins. As expected based on our data above, CRAMP1 showed distinctive enrichment at the large cluster of replication-dependent histone genes at chromosome 6 (HIST1) (Figure 4A). Across all genes, WT CRAMP1 predominantly associated with promoter regions indicative of a role in transcriptional regulation, displaying a strong enrichment at histone gene promoters (Figures 4B and 4C). Average signal intensities were markedly reduced for the ΔSANT but not the ΔGB-N mutant (Figure 4B), suggesting the SANT/Myb domain represents a primary DNA-binding determinant in CRAMP1. Disrupting GB domain integrity selectively abrogated CRAMP1 association with histone gene promoters and replicative histone gene clusters (Figures 4A–4C), consistent with NPAT- and GON4L-dependent CRAMP1 recruitment to HLBs via the GB domain. Interestingly, the association of CRAMP1 with H1 promoter regions showed a much broader distribution than at core histone promoters, and this association was abolished by loss of either the SANT/Myb or GB domain (Figures 4B and 4C). However, deletion of the SANT/Myb domain only abolished CRAMP1 association with H1 but not core histone promoter regions (Figures 4B and 4C), indicating that this domain specifically targets CRAMP1 to H1 promoter motifs. The peaks of CRAMP1 association with H1 promoters coincided with previously identified H1-specific promoter elements known as the CH1UE and H1 box motifs28 (Figures S8A and S8B). Notably, these motifs were uniquely absent from the H1.1 gene, which was bound by CRAMP1 in a SANT/Myb-independent manner and whose transcription is upregulated in CRAMP1-KO cells unlike other H1 isoforms (Figures 2E, 2H, 2I, 4C, S5A, and S8A). Moreover, the H1.0 and H1.10 promoters that are recognized in a SANT/Myb-dependent manner lack other motifs (CCAAT and TATA box, respectively) conserved among H1 promoters (Figure S8A). Taken together, these observations suggest that the CRAMP1 SANT/Myb domain is a reader of the CH1UE and/or H1 box motifs.Figure 4CRAMP1 functions as a histone H1 transcription factor(A) Hilbert curves showing CUT&RUN signal intensities on chromosome 6 for U2OS CRAMP1-KO/CRAMP1-GFP WT, ΔSANT, and ΔGB-N cells. Merged signal from 3 technical replicates, quantitated using spike-in normalized reads per million in 1 kb non-overlapping bins. Dashed lines highlight promoter regions of indicated genes.(D–F) Rank plots showing genes differentially expressed and differentially bound by CRAMP1 for indicated comparisons between U2OS WT, U2OS/CRAMP1-KO, and U2OS CRAMP1-KO/CRAMP1-GFP cell lines. Only differentially expressed genes (log2 fold change ≥ 0.58 and adjusted p values < 0.05) were included in the analysis. Genes were ranked based on the log10 rank product calculated using BETA.29(G) RT-qPCR analysis of H1.4 pre-mRNA levels relative to mature H1.4 transcript levels in U2OS cells transfected with indicated siRNAs (mean ± SEM; n = 3 biological replicates).(H) Schematic of CRAMP1 targeting to histone H1 promoters via concordant recognition of H1 promoter motifs by the SANT/Myb domain and recruitment to histone promoters via the GON4L-binding GB domain.The findings above suggested that CRAMP1 acts as a histone H1 transcription factor. To further investigate this, we integrated our RNA sequencing (RNA-seq) and CRAMP1 CUT&RUN datasets to pinpoint genes whose transcription is directly mediated by CRAMP1, i.e., genes whose promoters are bound by CRAMP1 and whose transcription is CRAMP1-dependent. This revealed prominent enrichment of H1 isoforms in a manner requiring both the SANT/Myb and GB domains (Figures 4D–4F), underscoring that linker histones are major targets of CRAMP1-dependent transcription mediated by these domains. Interestingly, the requirement of the GB domain, which links CRAMP1 to HLBs via GON4L and NPAT, for the expression of non-replicative H1 isoforms suggested that these key HLB components not only promote replicative histone biogenesis but are also instrumental for the transcription of replication-independent H1 genes. Consistent with this notion, depletion of NPAT reduced expression of both replicative and non-replicative H1 isoforms (Figures S5D–S5F). While CRAMP1 is required for the transcription of H1 isoforms, we established using qPCR analysis and primers specific for unprocessed and mature H1.4 transcripts30 that it is dispensable for H1 mRNA processing, unlike the stem-loop-processing factor SLBP (Figures 4G and S8C). Collectively, these findings suggest that CRAMP1 acts as an H1-specific histone transcription factor via a dual recruitment mode involving its concurrent targeting to all histone gene loci via GON4L binding by the GB domain and recognition of H1-specific promoter elements by the SANT/Myb domain (Figure 4H).CRAMP1 promotes TOP2i tolerance via histone H1 biogenesisPrompted by the findings above, we asked whether the H1 biogenesis role of CRAMP1 underlies its requirement for TOP2i tolerance. Supporting this, stably expressed CRAMP1 ΔSANT and ΔGB-N mutants that fail to stimulate H1 biogenesis both displayed deficiency in rectifying etoposide and ICRF-193 hypersensitivity as well as etoposide-induced hyper-accumulation of γH2AX and RPA2 foci in CRAMP1-KO cells, unlike CRAMP1 WT (Figures 5A, 5B, S9A, and S9B). Remarkably, extending these observations using base editing-driven deep mutational scanning to systematically map residues in CRAMP1 required for TOP2i resistance, we found that point mutations giving rise to a significant fitness defect in the presence of TOP2i mapped almost exclusively to the SANT/Myb and GB domains, displaying similar patterns in etoposide- and ICRF-193-treated cells (Figures 5C and 5D). This strongly suggests that the H1 biogenesis function represents the unifying key requirement of CRAMP1 in antagonizing the cytotoxicity of both TOP2 poisons and catalytic inhibitors. To confirm this, we overexpressed H1 isoforms in CRAMP1-depleted cells. Elevating H1.2 expression was sufficient to fully rescue the etoposide hypersensitivity of CRAMP1-KO cells and the accompanying increase in etoposide-induced γH2AX and RPA2 foci (Figures S9C–S9E). Importantly, similar effects were observed for a range of additional H1 isoforms including H1.1, H1.4, H1.0, and H1.10 (Figures 5E, 5F, and S9F), suggesting that depletion of the total H1 pool, rather than specific H1 isoforms, is the primary determinant of TOP2i hypersensitivity resulting from H1 deficiency. In support of this notion, we observed a notable correlation between the extent of H1 isoform dropout in our TOP2i CRISPR screens and their transcript abundance as determined by RNA-seq (Figures 5G and S9G). Furthermore, reducing the total H1 pool using an siRNA cocktail targeting a range of H1 isoforms31 hypersensitized cells to etoposide, similar to the impact of CRAMP1 loss (Figures 5H and S9H). We conclude that CRAMP1-dependent H1 synthesis is critically required for TOP2i resistance.Figure 5CRAMP1 promotes TOP2i tolerance via histone H1 biogenesis(A) SRB cell growth assays using U2OS/CRAMP1-KO and U2OS CRAMP1-KO/CRAMP1-GFP cell lines treated with various doses of etoposide (mean ± SEM; n = 3 independent experiments).(B) Quantitative immunofluorescence analysis of γH2AX mean intensity in U2OS WT, U2OS/CRAMP1-KO, and U2OS CRAMP1-KO/CRAMP1-GFP cell lines treated or not with etoposide (20 μM) for 1 h (mean ± SEM; n = 3 independent experiments; at least 5,000 cells analyzed per condition; unpaired two-tailed t test).(C) Schematic outline of base editor tiling screen for residues in human CRAMP1 required for etoposide or ICRF-193 tolerance.(D) Base editor tiling analysis of human CRAMP1, carried out as shown in (C). x axis shows amino acid position of CRAMP1. y axis displays log2 fold changes for etoposide (red) or IRCF-193 treatment (blue) relative to mock treatment. Each circle represents a single sgRNA. Large circles indicate sgRNAs with significant changes (p ≤ 0.01). Lower bar plot shows sequence conservation among metazoan CRAMP1 orthologs.(E) SRB cell growth assays using U2OS cells or a derivative cell line stably overexpressing indicated histone H1 isoforms (H1+) transfected with siRNAs and treated with various doses of etoposide (mean ± SEM; n = 3 independent experiments).(F) Quantitative immunofluorescence analysis of γH2AX mean intensity in cells in (E), collected after a 1-h treatment with etoposide (20 μM) (mean ± SEM; n = 3 independent experiments; at least 5,000 cells analyzed per condition; unpaired two-tailed t test).(G) Correlation analysis of NormZ values from the etoposide CRISPR-Cas9 screen (x axis) vs. RNA-seq transcript abundance (TPM) (y axis) for histone H1 isoforms in U2OS cells (mean ± SEM, n = 3 technical replicates). R2 value was calculated using linear regression.(H) SRB cell growth assays using U2OS cells transfected with non-targeting control (CTRL) siRNA or an siRNA pool targeting different H1 isoforms and treated with various doses of etoposide (mean ± SEM; n = 3 independent experiments).∗∗∗p < 0.001, ∗∗p < 0.01, ∗p < 0.05; ns, non-significant.Histone H1 deficiency drives TOP2i hypersensitization by increasing TOP2 demandThe findings above raised the question of how and why reduced H1 expression selectively hypersensitizes cells to TOP2i. Interestingly, we noticed that while overall TOP2 expression remained unchanged, TOP2 chromatin association increased significantly and, more importantly, was accompanied by a striking dispersal from its predominantly nucleolar enrichment32 to a pan-nuclear localization pattern upon CRAMP1 loss in otherwise unperturbed cells (Figures 6A, 6B, S10A, and S10B). This effect was seen for both TOP2A and TOP2B but not TOP1 and was phenocopied by H1 pool depletion (Figures 6A, 6B, and S10C–S10F). Moreover, the redistribution and excessive chromatin loading of TOP2A resulting from CRAMP1 ablation could be fully rescued by overexpression of different H1 isoforms or stably complemented CRAMP1 WT but not the H1 biogenesis-defective ΔSANT and ΔGB mutants (Figures 6A, 6B, and S10G–S10I). This suggests that reduced H1 expression and chromatin association, which decreases the compaction of low-accessibility chromatin states,5 generates new TOP2 binding sites that may underlie the observed increase in TOP2cc formation accompanied by DSBs upon etoposide treatment (Figures 1J and S2A–S2E). Consistent with this scenario, TOP2 CC-seq analysis33 of etoposide-induced genome-wide TOP2cc distribution revealed that while TOP2ccs predominantly accumulated in proximity to transcriptional start sites (TSS) in parental cells as expected,33 CRAMP1 deficiency led to a significantly increased level and much broader genomic distribution of TOP2ccs, which was fully reversed by stably expressed CRAMP1 (Figures 6C, S11A, and S11B). Notably, the CRAMP1 KO-induced gain in TOP2cc formation inversely correlated with chromatin accessibility as defined by ChromHMM,34 being particularly prominent for inaccessible chromatin states including heterochromatic, quiescent, and polycomb repressed regions (Figures 6D and S11A). In line with this, ATAC-seq analysis showed that CRAMP1 loss reduces nucleosome repeat length and chromatin compaction reminiscent of the reported impact of decreased H1 abundance5 (Figures S11C and S11D). Moreover, consistent with H1 deficiency-induced decompaction selectively impacting ChromHMM-defined low-accessibility chromatin states,5 TOP2cc signals showed a remarkable shift from accessible to inaccessible regions in CRAMP1-KO cells, denoting a strong increase in TOP2 catalytic action within highly compacted chromatin (Figures 6E and S11E). Notably, our RNA-seq data revealed that a large proportion of upregulated transcripts in CRAMP1-deficient cells map to heterochromatin (Figure 6F), suggesting that increased transcription-associated supercoiling in repressive chromatin states may be an important determinant underlying increased TOP2 usage in these regions upon impaired CRAMP1-dependent H1 biogenesis. Thus, loss of CRAMP1 triggers extensive unscheduled TOP2 redistribution to, and engagement in, chromatin states becoming more accessible and transcriptionally active upon reduction of the H1 pool.Figure 6Histone H1 deficiency drives TOP2i hypersensitivity by elevating TOP2 substrate formation and demand(A) Representative images of U2OS cells transfected sequentially with CRAMP1 siRNA and indicated GFP expression constructs and co-immunostained with TOP2A and fibrillarin antibodies. Scale bar, 10 μM.(B) Quantification of mean intensity of TOP2A (nucleolar/extranucleolar ratio) for the experiment in (A) (mean ± SEM; n = 3 biological replicates; at least 50 cells analyzed per condition).(C) Average of calibrated TOP2 CC-seq signals around protein-coding TSSs for U2OS WT, CRAMP1-KO, and CRAMP1-KO/CRAMP1-GFP cells (mean of n = 4 biological replicates).(D) Bar plot comparing fold change in TOP2 CC-seq signal densities in each ChromHMM-defined chromatin state, between U2OS WT, CRAMP1-KO, and CRAMP1-KO/CRAMP1-GFP cell lines. The relative accessibility of the chromatin states35 is indicated (mean ± SEM of n = 4 biological replicates).(E) TOP2 CC-seq coverage averaged per chromosome in regions with different chromatin accessibility defined using ATAC-seq signal in WT and CRAMP1-KO cells. Different ATAC-seq accessibility regions were defined using ChromHMM chromatin states split into three categories as in (D) (mean TOP2cc coverage from n = 2 biological replicates).(F) Venn diagram showing proportion of transcripts upregulated in CRAMP1-KO cells and corrected by stable complementation with CRAMP1-GFP that map to heterochromatic regions defined by ChromHMM (p = 6.04 × 10−22 when comparing to transcripts not regulated by CRAMP1 KO; Fisher’s exact test).(G) Immunoblot analysis of TOP2A expression in HCT116-TOP2A-mAID cells after treatment with indicated doses of auxin for 72 h.(H) SRB cell growth assays using HCT116-TOP2A-mAID transfected with control (CTRL) or CRAMP1 siRNAs and grown in presence of indicated auxin doses (mean ± SEM; n = 3 independent experiments).(I) As in (H), except cells were transfected with a pool of siRNAs targeting different H1 isoforms (mean ± SEM; n = 3 independent experiments).(J) Quantification of nuclear area (analyzed as in Figure 1I) in U2OS WT and CRAMP1-KO cells that were treated or not with etoposide (2 μM) for 1 h, transfected with GFP-TOP2A plasmid or empty vector 24 h later, and collected after an additional 48 h (mean ± SEM; n = 3 independent experiments; at least 2,000 cells analyzed per condition; unpaired two-tailed t test).(K) Incucyte analysis of cell area (μm2) of HCT116-TOP2A-mAID cells transfected with indicated siRNAs and grown in the presence of auxin (1.5 μM) (mean ± SEM; n = 3 independent experiments).∗∗∗p < 0.001, ∗∗p < 0.01, ∗p < 0.05; ns, non-significant.We reasoned that this dramatic TOP2 repositioning could signify an elevated total demand for TOP2 activity in cells with reduced H1 expression, due to the accumulation of TOP2 substrates in compacted chromatin states gaining accessibility and the concomitant depletion of TOP2 from its normal sites of action in open chromatin. This could explain the hypersensitivity of CRAMP1-deficient cells to disruption of TOP2 action by both poison-type and catalytic TOP2i. Such increased demand for TOP2 predicts that CRAMP1- or H1-depleted cells should also be more susceptible to reducing TOP2 expression in the absence of TOP2i. To test this, we used a degron cell line enabling auxin-induced partial TOP2A depletion36 (Figure 6G). We found that knockdown of CRAMP1 or H1 indeed exacerbated the fitness defect imposed by diminished TOP2A expression (Figures 6H and 6I), in agreement with TOP2 becoming more rate-limiting upon its aberrant chromatin accumulation and redistribution resulting from H1 undersupply. Likewise, equivalent to the impact of etoposide treatment (Figure 1H), auxin-induced downregulation of TOP2A led to elevated p21 expression and an increase in the proportion of enlarged cells that was augmented by CRAMP1 knockdown (Figures 6J and S11F). Conversely, overexpression of TOP2A after exposure to etoposide partially rescued the increase in cell size and hyper-accumulation of RPA2 foci in CRAMP1-KO cells (Figures 6K and S11G). These observations suggest that the enhanced TOP2i-driven induction of senescence-like features in CRAMP1-deficient cells is at least partially due to exhaustion of the TOP2 pool. We conclude that reduced H1 abundance undermines TOP2i tolerance by triggering extensive formation of TOP2 substrates in compacted chromatin states that gain accessibility, increasing total demand for TOP2 activity and thus lowering the threshold for cytotoxic impacts of TOP2 inhibition.

CRAMP1 is required for resistance to TOP2iTo discover factors protecting against TOP2i cytotoxicity, we performed parallel genome-scale CRISPR-Cas9 gene inactivation screens in human U2OS osteosarcoma cells for synthetic lethality relationships with the TOP2 poison etoposide and the catalytic TOP2i ICRF-193, which trap TOP2 on DNA in a covalent and non-covalent manner, respectively (Figure 1A).20 Along with well-established repair factors for trapped TOP2 complexes, including ZNF451 and TDP2,20 these screens revealed genes encoding the uncharacterized protein CRAMP1 and the histone H1 isoform H1.2 among a small subset displaying synthetic lethal interactions with both etoposide and ICRF-193 (Figures 1A–1C; Table S1). Additional H1 isoforms, including H1.0 and H1.4, scored just below the significance threshold (Figure 1B; Table S1). Targeted knockout (KO) of CRAMP1 in U2OS or HeLa cells, which did not alter cell cycle distribution in unstressed cells, recapitulated the strong hypersensitivity to etoposide and ICRF-193 resulting from CRAMP1 loss observed in the screens (Figures 1D and S1A–S1F). Importantly, this hypersensitivity could be fully rescued by stable complementation with wild-type (WT) GFP-tagged CRAMP1 (Figure 1E). Moreover, KO or small interfering RNA (siRNA)-mediated depletion of CRAMP1 selectively hypersensitized cells to TOP2i but not to a range of other genotoxins, including the topoisomerase 1 (TOP1) inhibitor camptothecin (Figures 1D, S1D, S1G, and S1H). TOP2 poisons, including etoposide and doxorubicin, potently trigger DNA double-strand break (DSB) formation by stabilizing covalent TOP2 cleavage complexes (TOP2ccs).20 Surprisingly, however, while CRAMP1-KO cells accumulated significantly elevated amounts of etoposide-induced DSBs as evidenced by γH2AX foci formation and neutral comet assays, they repaired these lesions with similar kinetics to that of parental cells, unlike depletion of TDP2, which significantly delayed etoposide-induced DSB clearance as expected23 (Figures 1F and S2A–S2F). This suggests that CRAMP1 does not function as a conventional repair factor for TOP2ccs. Indeed, CRAMP1 KO was non-epistatic with ZNF451 loss in promoting etoposide tolerance (Figures 1G and S2G). Despite CRAMP1-KO cells being proficient for resolution of etoposide-induced DSBs, they displayed an increased accumulation in G2 phase, a marked increase in the proportion of enlarged cells, and elevated p21 expression relative to their WT counterparts after etoposide treatment, reminiscent of a senescence-like state (Figures 1H, 1I, and S3A–S3C). DSB formation in human cells triggers a signaling cascade initiated by the ataxia telangiectasia mutated (ATM) kinase.24 Interestingly, however, etoposide-treated CRAMP1-KO cells showed a persistent increase in ATR-dependent RPA foci formation and RPA2 S33 phosphorylation but not markers of ATM signaling, including CHK2 T68 and KAP1 S824 phosphorylation (Figures 1J and S3D–S3F). ATR inhibition fully suppressed the etoposide-induced enlargement of CRAMP1-KO cells and increased occurrence of RPA foci (Figures 1I, 1J, and S3G). Moreover, CRAMP1-KO led to an increased level of mitotic DNA synthesis indicative of DNA under-replication25 after etoposide treatment (Figures S3H and S3I), suggesting that CRAMP1 deficiency induces a failure to complete DNA replication following exposure to etoposide, triggering sustained ATR-dependent cell cycle arrest in G2 phase. CRAMP1 KO likewise exacerbated G2 arrest and accumulation of large cells upon treatment with ICRF-193 that does not generate TOP2ccs (Figures S3J and S3K), substantiating that CRAMP1 does not act as a repair factor for these lesions. Together, these data show that CRAMP1 has a critical role in promoting resistance to TOP2i but is dispensable for resolving DNA damage induced by these agents.Figure 1CRAMP1 is required for TOP2i resistance but not repair of TOP2i-induced DNA damage(A) Schematic outline of genome-scale CRISPR-Cas9 screens for genes whose KO sensitizes cells to etoposide or ICRF-193. LD20, 20% lethal dose; NGS, next-generation sequencing.(B) DrugZ analysis of sgRNA depletion in U2OS cells following 12 days of etoposide or ICRF-193 treatment (n = 2 technical replicates). NormZ values of <−3 and >3 were used as cutoffs for defining significant gene KOs conferring hypersensitivity and resistance, respectively, to the drugs. See Table S1 for full results.(C) Venn diagram of significant genes (NormZ < −3) from DrugZ analysis of CRISPR screens in (B).(D) Sulforhodamine B (SRB) cell growth assays using U2OS WT and derivative CRAMP1-KO cell lines treated with various doses of TOP2i (etoposide, ICRF-193, and doxorubicin; top) or other genotoxic agents (bottom) (mean ± SEM; n = 3 independent experiments).(E) SRB cell growth assays using U2OS WT, CRAMP1-KO, and CRAMP1-KO/CRAMP1-GFP cell lines treated with various doses of etoposide (mean ± SEM; n = 3 independent experiments).(F) Quantitative immunofluorescence analysis of γH2AX mean intensity in U2OS WT and CRAMP1-KO cells treated with etoposide (20 μM) for 1 h and collected at indicated times (mean ± SEM; n = 5 independent experiments; at least 5,000 cells analyzed per condition; unpaired two-tailed t test).(G) SRB cell growth assays using U2OS WT and CRAMP1-KO cells transfected with non-targeting control (CTRL) or ZNF451 siRNAs and treated with indicated doses of etoposide (mean ± SEM; n = 3 independent experiments).(H) Representative images of U2OS WT and CRAMP1-KO cells treated or not with etoposide (2 μM) for 1 h and fixed 48 h later. Scale bar, 10 μM.(I) Quantification of nuclear area in U2OS WT and CRAMP1-KO cells that were left untreated or exposed to etoposide (2 μM) for 1 h, then collected immediately or grown for an additional 48 h in the absence or presence of ATR inhibitor (ATRi; 1 μM) (mean ± SEM; n = 3 independent experiments; at least 2,000 cells analyzed per condition; unpaired two-tailed t test). The maximal nuclear area in untreated cells was used as a cutoff to determine the proportion of cells with increased nuclear size.(J) Quantification of RPA2 foci in G2 phase cells in (I) immunostained with RPA2 antibody (mean ± SEM; n = 3 independent experiments; at least 500 cells analyzed per condition; unpaired two-tailed t test).∗∗∗p < 0.001, ∗∗p < 0.01, ∗p < 0.05; ns, non-significant.

CRAMP1 is required for resistance to TOP2i

To discover factors protecting against TOP2i cytotoxicity, we performed parallel genome-scale CRISPR-Cas9 gene inactivation screens in human U2OS osteosarcoma cells for synthetic lethality relationships with the TOP2 poison etoposide and the catalytic TOP2i ICRF-193, which trap TOP2 on DNA in a covalent and non-covalent manner, respectively (Figure 1A).20 Along with well-established repair factors for trapped TOP2 complexes, including ZNF451 and TDP2,20 these screens revealed genes encoding the uncharacterized protein CRAMP1 and the histone H1 isoform H1.2 among a small subset displaying synthetic lethal interactions with both etoposide and ICRF-193 (Figures 1A–1C; Table S1). Additional H1 isoforms, including H1.0 and H1.4, scored just below the significance threshold (Figure 1B; Table S1). Targeted knockout (KO) of CRAMP1 in U2OS or HeLa cells, which did not alter cell cycle distribution in unstressed cells, recapitulated the strong hypersensitivity to etoposide and ICRF-193 resulting from CRAMP1 loss observed in the screens (Figures 1D and S1A–S1F). Importantly, this hypersensitivity could be fully rescued by stable complementation with wild-type (WT) GFP-tagged CRAMP1 (Figure 1E). Moreover, KO or small interfering RNA (siRNA)-mediated depletion of CRAMP1 selectively hypersensitized cells to TOP2i but not to a range of other genotoxins, including the topoisomerase 1 (TOP1) inhibitor camptothecin (Figures 1D, S1D, S1G, and S1H). TOP2 poisons, including etoposide and doxorubicin, potently trigger DNA double-strand break (DSB) formation by stabilizing covalent TOP2 cleavage complexes (TOP2ccs).20 Surprisingly, however, while CRAMP1-KO cells accumulated significantly elevated amounts of etoposide-induced DSBs as evidenced by γH2AX foci formation and neutral comet assays, they repaired these lesions with similar kinetics to that of parental cells, unlike depletion of TDP2, which significantly delayed etoposide-induced DSB clearance as expected23 (Figures 1F and S2A–S2F). This suggests that CRAMP1 does not function as a conventional repair factor for TOP2ccs. Indeed, CRAMP1 KO was non-epistatic with ZNF451 loss in promoting etoposide tolerance (Figures 1G and S2G). Despite CRAMP1-KO cells being proficient for resolution of etoposide-induced DSBs, they displayed an increased accumulation in G2 phase, a marked increase in the proportion of enlarged cells, and elevated p21 expression relative to their WT counterparts after etoposide treatment, reminiscent of a senescence-like state (Figures 1H, 1I, and S3A–S3C). DSB formation in human cells triggers a signaling cascade initiated by the ataxia telangiectasia mutated (ATM) kinase.24 Interestingly, however, etoposide-treated CRAMP1-KO cells showed a persistent increase in ATR-dependent RPA foci formation and RPA2 S33 phosphorylation but not markers of ATM signaling, including CHK2 T68 and KAP1 S824 phosphorylation (Figures 1J and S3D–S3F). ATR inhibition fully suppressed the etoposide-induced enlargement of CRAMP1-KO cells and increased occurrence of RPA foci (Figures 1I, 1J, and S3G). Moreover, CRAMP1-KO led to an increased level of mitotic DNA synthesis indicative of DNA under-replication25 after etoposide treatment (Figures S3H and S3I), suggesting that CRAMP1 deficiency induces a failure to complete DNA replication following exposure to etoposide, triggering sustained ATR-dependent cell cycle arrest in G2 phase. CRAMP1 KO likewise exacerbated G2 arrest and accumulation of large cells upon treatment with ICRF-193 that does not generate TOP2ccs (Figures S3J and S3K), substantiating that CRAMP1 does not act as a repair factor for these lesions. Together, these data show that CRAMP1 has a critical role in promoting resistance to TOP2i but is dispensable for resolving DNA damage induced by these agents.Figure 1CRAMP1 is required for TOP2i resistance but not repair of TOP2i-induced DNA damage(A) Schematic outline of genome-scale CRISPR-Cas9 screens for genes whose KO sensitizes cells to etoposide or ICRF-193. CRAMP1 is an H1-specific histone biogenesis factorTo understand how CRAMP1 protects against TOP2i cytotoxicity, we first used mass spectrometry (MS) to profile the CRAMP1 interactome, using cells stably complemented with CRAMP1-GFP expressed at an approximately 8-fold higher level than endogenous CRAMP1 (Figures 2A and S4A). This revealed the poorly characterized protein GON4L as the most highly enriched candidate CRAMP1 interactor, which we validated in co-immunoprecipitation (coIP) assays (Figures 2B, S4B, and S4C; Table S2). GON4L has been linked to HLBs via interaction with NPAT,26,27 which was also significantly enriched in the CRAMP1 interactome along with FLASH (Figure 2B), suggesting an involvement of CRAMP1 in histone synthesis. This notion was reinforced by DepMap portal data showing a co-dependency signature for CRAMP1 that included linker histone isoforms and the histone chaperones ASF1 and HIRA (Figure S4D). Indeed, stably expressed CRAMP1-GFP showed distinctive enrichment in NPAT-positive HLBs throughout interphase (Figures 2C and 2D). CRAMP1 recruitment to HLBs was dependent on both GON4L and the HLB core components NPAT and FLASH, whereas CRAMP1 KO did not impair NPAT and FLASH association with HLBs (Figures 2C, 2D, and S4E–S4J). We thus asked whether CRAMP1 might be required for the expression of histone genes. Unexpectedly, considering its HLB enrichment, CRAMP1 KO markedly reduced the transcript and protein abundance of replicative linker histone isoforms including H1.2 and H1.4 but not core histones (Figures 2E–2H and S5A–S5C). By contrast, knockdown of NPAT or FLASH diminished both replicative H1 and core histone transcript levels as expected, while the impact of GON4L loss on histone biogenesis could not be rigorously assessed due to a drastic reduction of cell fitness upon siRNA-mediated depletion of GON4L that may reflect its common essentiality according to the DepMap portal (Figures S5D–S5G). Furthermore, CRAMP1 loss impaired both replicative and non-replicative H1 isoform expression, with the notable exception of H1.1, whose transcript and protein abundance were moderately upregulated in CRAMP1-KO cells (see below) (Figures 2E–2H, S5A, S5C, and S5H). The downregulation of H1 expression in CRAMP1-deficient cells was mirrored by a concomitant decline in the levels of chromatin-bound H1, as expected (Figures S5I and S5J). Across all histone transcripts, CRAMP1 ablation led to an approximately 50% reduction in total H1 expression but had no overt impact on core histone levels, paralleling MS-based quantification of histone protein abundance (Figures 2H, S5K, and S5L). Importantly, along with a number of other genes, defective expression of H1 isoforms in CRAMP1-KO cells was rescued by stable complementation with WT ectopic CRAMP1 (Figures 2I and S5M). We conclude from these findings that CRAMP1 defines a histone H1 biogenesis factor in human cells.Figure 2CRAMP1 is a histone H1 biogenesis factor(A) Schematic outline of MS-based analysis of CRAMP1-GFP-interacting proteins.(B) MS analysis of GFP pulldowns from U2OS Flp-In T-REx CRAMP1-KO (KO) cells stably reconstituted or not with CRAMP1-GFP (CRAMP1-KO/CRAMP1-GFP). Volcano plot shows the mean difference of the protein intensity plotted against the p value (two-tailed two-sample Student’s t test). Significant differences (q value < 0.05) were calculated by permutation-based false discovery rate (FDR) control (2,500 rounds of randomization) and are indicated in blue and red (n = 4 biological replicates). See Table S2 for full results.(C) Representative images of U2OS CRAMP1-KO/CRAMP1-GFP cells transfected with indicated siRNAs and immunostained with NPAT antibody. Scale bar, 10 μm.(D) Quantification of data in (C) (mean ± SEM; n = 3 independent experiments; at least 1,000 cells analyzed per condition; unpaired two-tailed t test).(E) RNA-seq heatmap displaying relative histone H1 transcript abundance calculated from normalized transcripts per million (TPM). CRAMP1 is an H1-specific histone biogenesis factor

To understand how CRAMP1 protects against TOP2i cytotoxicity, we first used mass spectrometry (MS) to profile the CRAMP1 interactome, using cells stably complemented with CRAMP1-GFP expressed at an approximately 8-fold higher level than endogenous CRAMP1 (Figures 2A and S4A). This revealed the poorly characterized protein GON4L as the most highly enriched candidate CRAMP1 interactor, which we validated in co-immunoprecipitation (coIP) assays (Figures 2B, S4B, and S4C; Table S2). GON4L has been linked to HLBs via interaction with NPAT,26,27 which was also significantly enriched in the CRAMP1 interactome along with FLASH (Figure 2B), suggesting an involvement of CRAMP1 in histone synthesis. This notion was reinforced by DepMap portal data showing a co-dependency signature for CRAMP1 that included linker histone isoforms and the histone chaperones ASF1 and HIRA (Figure S4D). Indeed, stably expressed CRAMP1-GFP showed distinctive enrichment in NPAT-positive HLBs throughout interphase (Figures 2C and 2D). CRAMP1 recruitment to HLBs was dependent on both GON4L and the HLB core components NPAT and FLASH, whereas CRAMP1 KO did not impair NPAT and FLASH association with HLBs (Figures 2C, 2D, and S4E–S4J). We thus asked whether CRAMP1 might be required for the expression of histone genes. Unexpectedly, considering its HLB enrichment, CRAMP1 KO markedly reduced the transcript and protein abundance of replicative linker histone isoforms including H1.2 and H1.4 but not core histones (Figures 2E–2H and S5A–S5C). By contrast, knockdown of NPAT or FLASH diminished both replicative H1 and core histone transcript levels as expected, while the impact of GON4L loss on histone biogenesis could not be rigorously assessed due to a drastic reduction of cell fitness upon siRNA-mediated depletion of GON4L that may reflect its common essentiality according to the DepMap portal (Figures S5D–S5G). Furthermore, CRAMP1 loss impaired both replicative and non-replicative H1 isoform expression, with the notable exception of H1.1, whose transcript and protein abundance were moderately upregulated in CRAMP1-KO cells (see below) (Figures 2E–2H, S5A, S5C, and S5H). The downregulation of H1 expression in CRAMP1-deficient cells was mirrored by a concomitant decline in the levels of chromatin-bound H1, as expected (Figures S5I and S5J). Across all histone transcripts, CRAMP1 ablation led to an approximately 50% reduction in total H1 expression but had no overt impact on core histone levels, paralleling MS-based quantification of histone protein abundance (Figures 2H, S5K, and S5L). Importantly, along with a number of other genes, defective expression of H1 isoforms in CRAMP1-KO cells was rescued by stable complementation with WT ectopic CRAMP1 (Figures 2I and S5M). We conclude from these findings that CRAMP1 defines a histone H1 biogenesis factor in human cells.Figure 2CRAMP1 is a histone H1 biogenesis factor(A) Schematic outline of MS-based analysis of CRAMP1-GFP-interacting proteins.(B) MS analysis of GFP pulldowns from U2OS Flp-In T-REx CRAMP1-KO (KO) cells stably reconstituted or not with CRAMP1-GFP (CRAMP1-KO/CRAMP1-GFP). Volcano plot shows the mean difference of the protein intensity plotted against the p value (two-tailed two-sample Student’s t test). Histone H1 biogenesis is mediated by a CRAMP1-GON4L-NPAT pathwayWe next addressed the mechanistic basis of CRAMP1-dependent H1 biogenesis. AlphaFold structure modeling predicted two folded domains in human CRAMP1, comprising an N-terminal putative SANT/Myb domain found in many transcriptional regulators and chromatin-binding proteins and a domain of unknown function that we refer to as GON4L binding (GB) composed of bipartite folds (GB-N and GB-C) separated by an extended linker (Figures 3A and S6A). Importantly, stable complementation of CRAMP1-KO cells with CRAMP1-GFP deletion mutants lacking the SANT/Myb domain (ΔSANT) or individual lobes of the GB domain (ΔGB-N or ΔGB-C) established that both domains are essential for CRAMP1-mediated H1 biogenesis (Figures 3B and S6B–S6D). Disrupting GB domain integrity abrogated CRAMP1 enrichment at HLBs, whereas deletion of the SANT/Myb domain had no effect (Figures 3C and 3D). In agreement with the GON4L dependency for CRAMP1 recruitment to HLBs, AlphaFold-Multimer analysis suggested with high confidence that the CRAMP1 GB domain interacts with a C-terminal portion of GON4L, involving an extensive binding interface (Figures 3E and S7A). Consistently, coIP analysis confirmed that the CRAMP1-GON4L interaction is mediated by these regions (Figures 3F, S7B, and S7C). Further in silico modeling suggested that GON4L directly bridges CRAMP1 with NPAT (Figures 3G, S7D, and S7E). This was corroborated by MS data showing selective dropout of GON4L and NPAT from the CRAMP1 interactome upon deletion of the GB but not the SANT/Myb domain (Figures 3H and S7F; Table S2), providing a mechanistic explanation for GON4L- and NPAT-dependent recruitment of CRAMP1 to HLBs via the GB domain. These findings show that CRAMP1 interaction with GON4L-NPAT and HLBs is essential for its role in stimulating H1 biogenesis.Figure 3Histone H1 biogenesis is mediated by a CRAMP1-GON4L-NPAT pathway(A) AlphaFold2 model (top) and domain structure (bottom) of human CRAMP1. Insets (right) show zoomed-in views of the SANT/Myb (dark purple) and GB (light purple) domains. Deletions introduced to generate the ΔSANT, ΔGB-N, and ΔGB-C mutants are indicated.(B) RNA-seq heatmap displaying relative histone H1 transcript abundance from normalized TPM in the indicated cellular backgrounds.(C) Representative images of U2OS CRAMP1-KO/CRAMP1-GFP cell lines immunostained with NPAT antibody. Scale bar, 10 μm.(D) Quantification of data in (C) (mean ± SEM; n = 3 independent experiments; at least 1,000 cells analyzed per condition; unpaired two-tailed t test).(E) AlphaFold2-Multimer model of the CRAMP1 (306–800)-GON4L (1455–2024) interaction.(F) Immunoblot analysis of GFP IPs from U2OS WT, U2OS/CRAMP1-KO, and U2OS CRAMP1-KO/CRAMP1-GFP cell lines transfected with FLAG-GON4L expression construct.(G) AlphaFold-Multimer model of the complex between CRAMP1, GON4L, and NPAT.(H) MS analysis of GFP pulldowns from U2OS CRAMP1-KO/CRAMP1-GFP vs. CRAMP1-KO/CRAMP1-GFP ΔGB-N cells. Volcano plot shows the mean difference of the protein intensity plotted against the p value (two-tailed two-sample Student’s t test). Significant differences (q value < 0.05) were calculated as in Figure 2B (n = 4 biological replicates).See Table S2 for full results. ∗∗∗p < 0.001; ns, non-significant.

We next addressed the mechanistic basis of CRAMP1-dependent H1 biogenesis. AlphaFold structure modeling predicted two folded domains in human CRAMP1, comprising an N-terminal putative SANT/Myb domain found in many transcriptional regulators and chromatin-binding proteins and a domain of unknown function that we refer to as GON4L binding (GB) composed of bipartite folds (GB-N and GB-C) separated by an extended linker (Figures 3A and S6A). CRAMP1 acts as a histone H1 transcription factorBecause the CRAMP1 ΔSANT mutant showed intact recruitment to HLBs yet failed to restore expression of H1 isoforms in CRAMP1-KO cells, we speculated that the SANT/Myb domain might specifically target CRAMP1 to H1 gene loci. To test this, we used CUT&RUN to profile genome-wide DNA interactions of stably expressed GFP-CRAMP1 proteins. As expected based on our data above, CRAMP1 showed distinctive enrichment at the large cluster of replication-dependent histone genes at chromosome 6 (HIST1) (Figure 4A). Across all genes, WT CRAMP1 predominantly associated with promoter regions indicative of a role in transcriptional regulation, displaying a strong enrichment at histone gene promoters (Figures 4B and 4C). Average signal intensities were markedly reduced for the ΔSANT but not the ΔGB-N mutant (Figure 4B), suggesting the SANT/Myb domain represents a primary DNA-binding determinant in CRAMP1. Disrupting GB domain integrity selectively abrogated CRAMP1 association with histone gene promoters and replicative histone gene clusters (Figures 4A–4C), consistent with NPAT- and GON4L-dependent CRAMP1 recruitment to HLBs via the GB domain. Interestingly, the association of CRAMP1 with H1 promoter regions showed a much broader distribution than at core histone promoters, and this association was abolished by loss of either the SANT/Myb or GB domain (Figures 4B and 4C). However, deletion of the SANT/Myb domain only abolished CRAMP1 association with H1 but not core histone promoter regions (Figures 4B and 4C), indicating that this domain specifically targets CRAMP1 to H1 promoter motifs. The peaks of CRAMP1 association with H1 promoters coincided with previously identified H1-specific promoter elements known as the CH1UE and H1 box motifs28 (Figures S8A and S8B). Notably, these motifs were uniquely absent from the H1.1 gene, which was bound by CRAMP1 in a SANT/Myb-independent manner and whose transcription is upregulated in CRAMP1-KO cells unlike other H1 isoforms (Figures 2E, 2H, 2I, 4C, S5A, and S8A). Moreover, the H1.0 and H1.10 promoters that are recognized in a SANT/Myb-dependent manner lack other motifs (CCAAT and TATA box, respectively) conserved among H1 promoters (Figure S8A). Taken together, these observations suggest that the CRAMP1 SANT/Myb domain is a reader of the CH1UE and/or H1 box motifs.Figure 4CRAMP1 functions as a histone H1 transcription factor(A) Hilbert curves showing CUT&RUN signal intensities on chromosome 6 for U2OS CRAMP1-KO/CRAMP1-GFP WT, ΔSANT, and ΔGB-N cells. Merged spike-in normalized reads per million signals from n = 3 technical replicates.(B) CUT&RUN signal intensity (spike-in normalized reads per million [RPM]) in U2OS CRAMP1-KO cell lines stably expressing CRAMP1-GFP WT, ΔSANT, or ΔGB-N around TSSs for all genes (left), core histone genes (center), or H1 genes (right) (mean signal in n = 3 technical replicates).(C) CUT&RUN profiles of U2OS CRAMP1-KO/CRAMP1-GFP WT, ΔSANT, and ΔGB-N cells at different histone gene loci. Merged signal from 3 technical replicates, quantitated using spike-in normalized reads per million in 1 kb non-overlapping bins. Dashed lines highlight promoter regions of indicated genes.(D–F) Rank plots showing genes differentially expressed and differentially bound by CRAMP1 for indicated comparisons between U2OS WT, U2OS/CRAMP1-KO, and U2OS CRAMP1-KO/CRAMP1-GFP cell lines. Only differentially expressed genes (log2 fold change ≥ 0.58 and adjusted p values < 0.05) were included in the analysis. Genes were ranked based on the log10 rank product calculated using BETA.29(G) RT-qPCR analysis of H1.4 pre-mRNA levels relative to mature H1.4 transcript levels in U2OS cells transfected with indicated siRNAs (mean ± SEM; n = 3 biological replicates).(H) Schematic of CRAMP1 targeting to histone H1 promoters via concordant recognition of H1 promoter motifs by the SANT/Myb domain and recruitment to histone promoters via the GON4L-binding GB domain.The findings above suggested that CRAMP1 acts as a histone H1 transcription factor. To further investigate this, we integrated our RNA sequencing (RNA-seq) and CRAMP1 CUT&RUN datasets to pinpoint genes whose transcription is directly mediated by CRAMP1, i.e., genes whose promoters are bound by CRAMP1 and whose transcription is CRAMP1-dependent. This revealed prominent enrichment of H1 isoforms in a manner requiring both the SANT/Myb and GB domains (Figures 4D–4F), underscoring that linker histones are major targets of CRAMP1-dependent transcription mediated by these domains. Interestingly, the requirement of the GB domain, which links CRAMP1 to HLBs via GON4L and NPAT, for the expression of non-replicative H1 isoforms suggested that these key HLB components not only promote replicative histone biogenesis but are also instrumental for the transcription of replication-independent H1 genes. Consistent with this notion, depletion of NPAT reduced expression of both replicative and non-replicative H1 isoforms (Figures S5D–S5F). While CRAMP1 is required for the transcription of H1 isoforms, we established using qPCR analysis and primers specific for unprocessed and mature H1.4 transcripts30 that it is dispensable for H1 mRNA processing, unlike the stem-loop-processing factor SLBP (Figures 4G and S8C). Collectively, these findings suggest that CRAMP1 acts as an H1-specific histone transcription factor via a dual recruitment mode involving its concurrent targeting to all histone gene loci via GON4L binding by the GB domain and recognition of H1-specific promoter elements by the SANT/Myb domain (Figure 4H).

CRAMP1 acts as a histone H1 transcription factor

Because the CRAMP1 ΔSANT mutant showed intact recruitment to HLBs yet failed to restore expression of H1 isoforms in CRAMP1-KO cells, we speculated that the SANT/Myb domain might specifically target CRAMP1 to H1 gene loci. To test this, we used CUT&RUN to profile genome-wide DNA interactions of stably expressed GFP-CRAMP1 proteins. As expected based on our data above, CRAMP1 showed distinctive enrichment at the large cluster of replication-dependent histone genes at chromosome 6 (HIST1) (Figure 4A). Across all genes, WT CRAMP1 predominantly associated with promoter regions indicative of a role in transcriptional regulation, displaying a strong enrichment at histone gene promoters (Figures 4B and 4C). Average signal intensities were markedly reduced for the ΔSANT but not the ΔGB-N mutant (Figure 4B), suggesting the SANT/Myb domain represents a primary DNA-binding determinant in CRAMP1. Disrupting GB domain integrity selectively abrogated CRAMP1 association with histone gene promoters and replicative histone gene clusters (Figures 4A–4C), consistent with NPAT- and GON4L-dependent CRAMP1 recruitment to HLBs via the GB domain. Interestingly, the association of CRAMP1 with H1 promoter regions showed a much broader distribution than at core histone promoters, and this association was abolished by loss of either the SANT/Myb or GB domain (Figures 4B and 4C). However, deletion of the SANT/Myb domain only abolished CRAMP1 association with H1 but not core histone promoter regions (Figures 4B and 4C), indicating that this domain specifically targets CRAMP1 to H1 promoter motifs. The peaks of CRAMP1 association with H1 promoters coincided with previously identified H1-specific promoter elements known as the CH1UE and H1 box motifs28 (Figures S8A and S8B). Notably, these motifs were uniquely absent from the H1.1 gene, which was bound by CRAMP1 in a SANT/Myb-independent manner and whose transcription is upregulated in CRAMP1-KO cells unlike other H1 isoforms (Figures 2E, 2H, 2I, 4C, S5A, and S8A). Moreover, the H1.0 and H1.10 promoters that are recognized in a SANT/Myb-dependent manner lack other motifs (CCAAT and TATA box, respectively) conserved among H1 promoters (Figure S8A). The findings above suggested that CRAMP1 acts as a histone H1 transcription factor. To further investigate this, we integrated our RNA sequencing (RNA-seq) and CRAMP1 CUT&RUN datasets to pinpoint genes whose transcription is directly mediated by CRAMP1, i.e., genes whose promoters are bound by CRAMP1 and whose transcription is CRAMP1-dependent. This revealed prominent enrichment of H1 isoforms in a manner requiring both the SANT/Myb and GB domains (Figures 4D–4F), underscoring that linker histones are major targets of CRAMP1-dependent transcription mediated by these domains. Interestingly, the requirement of the GB domain, which links CRAMP1 to HLBs via GON4L and NPAT, for the expression of non-replicative H1 isoforms suggested that these key HLB components not only promote replicative histone biogenesis but are also instrumental for the transcription of replication-independent H1 genes. Consistent with this notion, depletion of NPAT reduced expression of both replicative and non-replicative H1 isoforms (Figures S5D–S5F). While CRAMP1 is required for the transcription of H1 isoforms, we established using qPCR analysis and primers specific for unprocessed and mature H1.4 transcripts30 that it is dispensable for H1 mRNA processing, unlike the stem-loop-processing factor SLBP (Figures 4G and S8C). Collectively, these findings suggest that CRAMP1 acts as an H1-specific histone transcription factor via a dual recruitment mode involving its concurrent targeting to all histone gene loci via GON4L binding by the GB domain and recognition of H1-specific promoter elements by the SANT/Myb domain (Figure 4H).

CRAMP1 promotes TOP2i tolerance via histone H1 biogenesisPrompted by the findings above, we asked whether the H1 biogenesis role of CRAMP1 underlies its requirement for TOP2i tolerance. Supporting this, stably expressed CRAMP1 ΔSANT and ΔGB-N mutants that fail to stimulate H1 biogenesis both displayed deficiency in rectifying etoposide and ICRF-193 hypersensitivity as well as etoposide-induced hyper-accumulation of γH2AX and RPA2 foci in CRAMP1-KO cells, unlike CRAMP1 WT (Figures 5A, 5B, S9A, and S9B). Remarkably, extending these observations using base editing-driven deep mutational scanning to systematically map residues in CRAMP1 required for TOP2i resistance, we found that point mutations giving rise to a significant fitness defect in the presence of TOP2i mapped almost exclusively to the SANT/Myb and GB domains, displaying similar patterns in etoposide- and ICRF-193-treated cells (Figures 5C and 5D). This strongly suggests that the H1 biogenesis function represents the unifying key requirement of CRAMP1 in antagonizing the cytotoxicity of both TOP2 poisons and catalytic inhibitors. To confirm this, we overexpressed H1 isoforms in CRAMP1-depleted cells. Elevating H1.2 expression was sufficient to fully rescue the etoposide hypersensitivity of CRAMP1-KO cells and the accompanying increase in etoposide-induced γH2AX and RPA2 foci (Figures S9C–S9E). Importantly, similar effects were observed for a range of additional H1 isoforms including H1.1, H1.4, H1.0, and H1.10 (Figures 5E, 5F, and S9F), suggesting that depletion of the total H1 pool, rather than specific H1 isoforms, is the primary determinant of TOP2i hypersensitivity resulting from H1 deficiency. In support of this notion, we observed a notable correlation between the extent of H1 isoform dropout in our TOP2i CRISPR screens and their transcript abundance as determined by RNA-seq (Figures 5G and S9G). Furthermore, reducing the total H1 pool using an siRNA cocktail targeting a range of H1 isoforms31 hypersensitized cells to etoposide, similar to the impact of CRAMP1 loss (Figures 5H and S9H). We conclude that CRAMP1-dependent H1 synthesis is critically required for TOP2i resistance.Figure 5CRAMP1 promotes TOP2i tolerance via histone H1 biogenesis(A) SRB cell growth assays using U2OS/CRAMP1-KO and U2OS CRAMP1-KO/CRAMP1-GFP cell lines treated with various doses of etoposide (mean ± SEM; n = 3 independent experiments).(B) Quantitative immunofluorescence analysis of γH2AX mean intensity in U2OS WT, U2OS/CRAMP1-KO, and U2OS CRAMP1-KO/CRAMP1-GFP cell lines treated or not with etoposide (20 μM) for 1 h (mean ± SEM; n = 3 independent experiments; at least 5,000 cells analyzed per condition; unpaired two-tailed t test).(C) Schematic outline of base editor tiling screen for residues in human CRAMP1 required for etoposide or ICRF-193 tolerance.(D) Base editor tiling analysis of human CRAMP1, carried out as shown in (C). x axis shows amino acid position of CRAMP1. y axis displays log2 fold changes for etoposide (red) or IRCF-193 treatment (blue) relative to mock treatment. Each circle represents a single sgRNA. Large circles indicate sgRNAs with significant changes (p ≤ 0.01). Lower bar plot shows sequence conservation among metazoan CRAMP1 orthologs.(E) SRB cell growth assays using U2OS cells or a derivative cell line stably overexpressing indicated histone H1 isoforms (H1+) transfected with siRNAs and treated with various doses of etoposide (mean ± SEM; n = 3 independent experiments).(F) Quantitative immunofluorescence analysis of γH2AX mean intensity in cells in (E), collected after a 1-h treatment with etoposide (20 μM) (mean ± SEM; n = 3 independent experiments; at least 5,000 cells analyzed per condition; unpaired two-tailed t test).(G) Correlation analysis of NormZ values from the etoposide CRISPR-Cas9 screen (x axis) vs. RNA-seq transcript abundance (TPM) (y axis) for histone H1 isoforms in U2OS cells (mean ± SEM, n = 3 technical replicates). R2 value was calculated using linear regression.(H) SRB cell growth assays using U2OS cells transfected with non-targeting control (CTRL) siRNA or an siRNA pool targeting different H1 isoforms and treated with various doses of etoposide (mean ± SEM; n = 3 independent experiments).∗∗∗p < 0.001, ∗∗p < 0.01, ∗p < 0.05; ns, non-significant.
